Memo To Musk: Denial Is Just The First Step On The Long Road To Recovery

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tesla CEO Elon Musk made headlines yet again last week, when he publicly denied any affiliation with US President Donald Trump’s “MAGA” movement. If Musk intends to stage a sort of political recovery, he still has a long way to go. Denial is just a precursor to recovery, which begins in earnest when you stop denying the reality of your situation and start admitting you need help from a higher power or some such. And in fact, a higher power is coming for Musk —  if not in spiritual form, then in the form of Congress, where the incoming Democratic majority plans to exercise its authority, and call Musk to account for manipulating SpaceX satellite services in Ukraine.

During an interview for the New York Times DealBook Summit last week, Elon Musk claimed that he was not affiliated with MAGA, though he did not apologize for supporting MAGA candidates this year, or for supporting Donald Trump in 2024. Musk simply said he “felt foolish” for wasting his money on losers.

In the same interview, Musk insisted — falsely — that Russia was the aggrieved party when Russian President Vladimir Putin invaded the Donbas region of Ukraine back in 2014. Justin sayin’…

For those of you wondering why Elon Musk’s politics should be a matter of concern to CleanTechnica, that’s a good question, and here’s the answer: Elon Musk sailed into clean tech history on the coattails of the EV startup Tesla Motors (soon to become simply Tesla) in the early 2000’s, only to emerge in more recent years as a champion of the far right alongside President Donald Trump’s notoriously racist, fossil fuel-supporting “Make America Great Again” movement, thereby setting back the US decarbonization movement by years if not decades.

Musk has also made his own contribution to the resurgence of fossil fuels in the US. His new xAI data centers are well known for deploying loosely regulated (or illegal) on-site gas generators, and his SpaceX venture has been gobbling up natural gas for rocket fuel while laying plans for future expansion.

So much for Elon Musk’s footprint on the US energy profile. Let’s move over to Ukraine, where rich fossil energy and mineral resources in the nation’s Donbas region prompted Russian President Putin to send unmarked soldiers — aka the “little green men” — to annex that portion of Ukraine in 2014. A ceasefire temporarily calmed the situation until February of 2022, when Putin sent a fully equipped convoy to the Ukrainian capital city Kyiv, aiming to take over the entire seat of government in one fell swoop — literally, in one day.

The effort failed, both militarily and in terms of Putin’s energy goals. Ukraine promptly cut Russia off from its electricity grid and hooked up with the European grid, a complex effort assisted by the Biden administration. Ukraine also set the wheels in motion to join the European Union as a significant source of clean energy, including green hydrogen gas alongside its copious potential for clean power including biomass, wind, and solar power.

To put that into perspective, consider that Ukraine is almost 90% as large as Texas, which has staked out a leadership position in wind and solar power among the 50 US states.

With Ukraine as a leading renewable energy supplier, the EU would have a fresh opportunity to meets its decarbonization goals on schedule while also reducing imports of fossil energy from Russia… And… Well… That’s where Elon Musk enters the picture.

Musk first emerged as a full blown MAGA supporter in 2020, when he helped Trump stir up conspiracy theories and confusion over the federal response to the COVID-19 crisis, in a failed effort to support the President’s bid for re-election that year. Had Trump won, he would have continued to implement a key part of his foreign policy agenda, namely, withdrawing the US from NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Alliance. NATO formed in 1949 as an effort to curb Russian expansion in Europe, an effort Trump has upended on account of his longstanding friendship with Putin.

The election of Joe Biden in 2020 did not prevent Musk from providing additional aid and comfort to Trump. Musk used his media-grabbing public profile to undercut the authority of President Biden at every opportunity, including on the world stage as well as here at home. The stream of insults extended into January of 2022, even as the reality of a fresh round of Russian expansion into Europe began to materialize.

Musk had an additional opportunity to challenge Biden’s authority in January of 2022, when the so-called “Freedom Convoy” of truckers barricaded government offices in Canada, ostensibly in protest of a vaccine mandate. Musk gleefully cheered the effort, which disrupted transportation systems and distracted public attention in two key NATO allies — Canada and the US — even as Russia began ratcheting up its plans to take over Kyiv.

However, once it became clear that Putin’s one-day takeover of Ukraine was a failure, Musk about-faced. He took credit for providing “free” communications aid to Ukraine in the form of satellite services through the Starlink branch of SpaceX.

JK! A later assessment revealed that other sources shouldered about 85% of the cost of Starlink terminals initially supplied to Ukraine, including USAID here in the US (not a typo — that’s the US Administration for International Development, the same agency that Musk later destroyed in 2025).

Poland and other groups also contributed initial assistance to purchase  the terminals alongside an estimated 30% of connectivity costs. In addition, by September of 2022 Musk was making the case for US taxpayers to foot the bill.

Don’t just take my word for it. In 2023, the Belfer Center for International Affairs at Harvard recapped SpaceX’s negotiations over payment for its services to Ukraine during the previous year, concluding that “we can assume the U.S. government agreed to pay some, if not all, of the costs for continued operation of the technology.”

“SpaceX, and particularly Musk, was able to play the role of wartime hero, showcasing the power and value of the technology products the company developed, while simultaneously avoiding sustained and hefty bills,” Belfer Center observed.

US Department of Defense contract or not, by 2023 SpaceX was already making unilateral decisions that restricted Ukraine’s ability to deploy the technology, with SpaceX CEO Gwynne Shotwell publicly insisting that the company never intended for Ukraine to use it “for offensive purposes.”

Of course, “offensive” is in the eye of the beholder. Since Ukraine is the invaded party seeking to assert its rights over internationally recognized borders against Russia, one could argue that every action Ukraine takes is a defensive one until Putin agrees to concede defeat and cede back his illegal claims over Ukrainian lands.

Of course, that’s not how Musk portrayed the situation in his DealBook interview last week. Nevertheless, one also could argue that the US government, not the vendor, controls the use of contractually obligated resources. Be that as it may, Musk raised the bar considerably in October, when he — or Trump — or both — once again dialed down satellite service in Ukraine, exposing that country to further attacks.

Musk attempted to mollify bipartisan outrage over the debacle with yet another publicity stunt for SpaceX. However, service interruptions have continued into this week. Meanwhile, Trump has spent the same week cozying up to both Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping, aided and abetted by an obedient Republican majority in Congress.

That majority will evaporate, though, in January 3, which is just around the corner. The incoming Democratic majority is already gearing up for a fight. In a letter dated November 20, the incoming Chair of the House Intelligence Committee, Jim Himes (D-CT), demanded an in-person briefing from senior SpaceX officers, seeking an explanation for the disruption of military-grade Starshield service in Ukraine. As leverage, Himes warned Musk that a Democratic majority in Congress will do what the Republican majority failed to do, namely, exercise its constitutional authority over federal contracts.

Referring to specifically to SpaceX’s military-grade Starshield satellite services in Ukraine, Himes warned Musk that his entire suite of present and future SpaceX security contracts with the US government is at risk. “SpaceX’s potentially unilateral modification of a U.S. Government contract, combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight, calls into question every contract the U.S. Government has with SpaceX,” Himes explained.

“For that reason, it is difficult for me to continue supporting the authorization of funding for these SpaceX- developed, government-owned systems until I receive satisfactory answers to my questions about the Starshield service in Ukraine,” Himes continued.

Will Musk simply ignore the request for a briefing? After all, as Himes notes, Musk failed to respond to earlier requests. Stay tuned…

Do You Have to File Taxes? Your Income Threshold and a Few Triggers Decide

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

You must file a 2026 federal return if your gross income reaches the threshold for your filing status and age, or if a trigger like $400 of net self-employment earnings applies. Otherwise filing is optional, but do it anyway if income tax was withheld from your pay, since only a return gets it back.

For most filers, the threshold is the standard deduction for their status and age, the amount subtracted from income before any tax is figured. If someone can claim you as a dependent, different limits apply.

For 2026 returns, filed in 2027, you must file once gross income reaches:

Gross income is all income that isn’t exempt from tax. Wages, tips, taxable interest, and unemployment benefits count, and so does a home sale’s gain, even if you can exclude it from tax. Social Security benefits count only in part, and only once half of them plus your other income, tax-exempt interest included, passes $25,000, or $32,000 on a joint return. Married people filing separately who lived together during the year count that part at any income.

These require a return whatever your income:

The IRS’s Interactive Tax Assistant asks about your filing status, withholding, and income, then says whether you must file or should file for a refund.

Say you’re single, under 65, and earned $12,000 part time in 2026, with $700 of federal income tax withheld. You owe no income tax and don’t have to file, but only a return gets the $700 back. Refundable credits such as the Earned Income Tax Credit also pay out only to filers.

A return filed within three years of its due date still gets its withholding back, so a skipped 2023 return has until mid-April 2027.

GTA 6 “Greatest Artistic Achievement In History,” Says Cyberpunk 2 Director

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Grand Theft Auto 6 is the highest-rated game in history, only rivaled by Ocarina of Time — but no developer should try to rival its scope and ambition.

We don't know what exactly the budget was, but Rockstar head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe recently pushed back on rumors of a $2 billion price tag, clarifying it cost somewhere under $1.5 billion. Most studios don't have that kind of change to throw around, because most studios aren't printing money with a game like GTA Online.

Indeed, Cyberpunk 2 creative director Igor Sarzynski has made it clear that CD Projekt Red has no intention of rivaling GTA 6, despite the broad-stroke similarities between both series.

"GTA 6-induced-open-world-game-dev-depression is real," he mused on Bluesky. "Probably the single greatest artistic achievement in history, I mean it. Scale, density, coherence, thoughtfulness of it all, just a technological and creative miracle. Cyberpunk 2 is canceled, imma go sweep autumn leaves in my backyard now.

"Seriously though I think we're doing something of our own, with a different unique flavor," he continued. "GTA is impressive in a way all great art is, it expands what is possible, a captivating vision flawlessly executed."

It's a sentiment echoed by others in the games' industry, like Larian Studios head of publishing Michael Douse, better known by his online moniker - Very AFK.

"Maybe in a world that often feels like it's going to sh*t it is nice for everyone to enjoy a cultural moment together that brings every audience (and every generation) the same excitement, behind something that has turned out to be genuinely brilliant," he posted to Twitter. "Not every game needs to be GTA, and that both makes space for other types of experiences, and the rare moment even more special!"

Naughty Dog game designer Michael Barclay even said that GTA 6 appears to him "as a Bugatti Veyron would to a nervous Victorian child."

Considering, just three years ago, the industry warned that Baldur's Gate 3 was the game no other developer should try to replicate, given the unbelievable scope and sheer amount of interactivity, recorded dialogue, and branching paths it offered to players, that's one hell of an endorsement.

TheGamer's own Lead News Editor, George Foster, put it bluntly in his GTA 6 review, "we'll never get a game like it again," and frankly, we shouldn't.

GTA 6 has reportedly been pre-ordered almost ten millions times.

Ballooning development budgets are already putting a target on studios' backs, making every single new release a financial gamble that could mean mass layoffs or even closure, and those high-stakes are only making the industry increasingly risk-averse.

GTA 6 shouldn't set a new bar for other developers to strive for. The unimaginable amount of resources, personnel, and time it took to develop the game is simply unrealistic for other studios. Thankfully, CDPR understands that. Let's just hope the rest of the industry does, too.

The most anticipated video game of all time is a triumph on every level that exceeds years of unbelievable expectations. You're not ready for Leonida.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

4 iPad Pro alternatives for those looking to ditch Apple

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple's iPad Pro is far from the only game in town.

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

If you're looking for the best tablet on the market  — or at least the best with a mobile operating system  — it is undoubtedly the 2025 iPad Pro. The latest iPad Pro sports Apple's M5 chip, which powered the previous year's MacBook Pro. It's a chip so powerful that most users are unlikely to brush against its performance ceiling. The tandem OLED display is similarly overkill, and is a feature no other tablet available in North America has. Apple is so far ahead of its competitors in the high-end tablet market that it feels like Tim Cook wanted to taunt them with its existence.

But the iPad Pro is not perfect. The locked-down iPadOS experience means you're limited to the apps Apple approves of. It also means you cannot install apps without signing into an Apple account. If you don't own other Apple devices, things can get awkward as the iPad becomes siloed away from the rest of your gadgets, requiring you to find workarounds for syncing files or notifications.

Thankfully, things are getting more interesting for tablets outside of Apple's walled garden. While no Android tablets can rival the iPad Pro's performance, many offer other advantages such as ecosystem synergy with Windows PCs, gaming features, expandable storage or more included accessories. And if performance is what you're after, it's worth a gander at Windows tablets now that Windows on ARM is coming into its own.

The easiest alternative to recommend is Samsung's newest flagship tablet, the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra. The first thing you'll notice is its size: it looks like someone tore the screen off a full-sized laptop, with a 14.6-inch Dynamic AMOLED 2x display that dwarfs even the larger 13-inch iPad Pro. If you love watching videos on your tablet, this is the only device that can give the iPad's luxurious tandem OLED display a run for its money. It also has Samsung's anti-reflective coating, which the iPad lacks.

What you won't get with the Galaxy tablet, or with any Android tablet, is the iPad Pro's raw horsepower. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra's MediaTek Dimensity 9500 processor is decently powerful, but it's nowhere near the desktop-class Apple M5 chip in the latest iPad Pro. That said, the Galaxy has more than enough firepower for games, video editing, and other taxing tasks, and it comes with 12GB of RAM (or 16GB on the 1TB storage version) to help get the job done.

Samsung's One UI is widely considered the best Android skin and an excellent alternative to iOS or iPadOS. It also has several features you won't find elsewhere in the Android space. If you have a Windows PC, One UI lets you use the tablet as a second PC display, for example, or share your mouse and keyboard with your computer.

Despite its reduced performance, the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra also competes with the iPad Pro on price. It starts at $1,400 for the 256GB model with 12GB of RAM and only gets steeper from there, maxing out at $2,000 for the 1TB model with 16GB of RAM.

Until recently, Amazon made cheap, underpowered Fire tablets meant mostly for basic entertainment. The new Alexa Tablet 12 Pro bucks that trend. Alexa Tablets don't compare that favorably to the iPad Pro, but they're cheaper and make reasonable compromises. Amazon has also ditched its Fire OS Android skin in favor of a more traditional Android experience — crucially, with access to the Google Play Store.

Amazon's head of hardware, Panos Panay, told Engadget the screen was the one component of the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro the company refused to compromise on. It has a slightly higher pixel density than the iPad Pro, with an optional matte coating similar to certain Samsung devices and Apple's iPhone Duo. Our review of the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro found the MediaTek Dimensity 8400 chip inside presented no obstacles to productivity and entertainment tasks, but did stutter a bit while multitasking. You get eight and 16GB of RAM on the 128- and 256GB storage models, respectively.

If price is what turns you off from the iPad, Amazon's starting price of $500 will be very appealing. However, that's for just 128GB of storage, which will fill up quickly since it lacks a microSD card expansion slot. The Nanomatte display is an extra $50. If you want the top-spec 256GB model, expect to pay $680, with the Nanomatte display included.

If you want a powerful tablet with gaming-focused features at a more affordable price than the iPad Pro, look no further than the RedMagic Astra 2. Part of that lower price, though, is because the Astra 2 has a 9-inch screen, 2 inches smaller than the smallest iPad Pro. It makes sense for gaming  — a large tablet can be tiring to hold up  — but if you want a large screen for videos or productivity, this may not be the device for you.

The Astra 2 runs the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5, making it arguably the most powerful Android tablet on this list, but still far short of Apple's M5 chip. The biggest barrier to consistent performance on mobile chips is cooling: Most Android devices with top-end chips throttle them to prevent overheating, but RedMagic compensates with a large vapor chamber and real liquid cooling to dissipate heat. GSMArena benchmarks show that the cooling gives the Astra 2 a slight advantage over the OnePlus Pad 4, which uses the same Snapdragon chip.

RedMagic has equipped the Astra 2 with a few bells and whistles Apple doesn't match. The display, though smaller, is more pixel-dense at 313 ppi vs. the iPad Pro's 264, while its 185Hz refresh rate also beats the iPad Pro's 120 (the newly updated iPad Mini is still stuck at 60Hz). It's a bit brighter, too. All of this enables a better gaming experience, which emphasizes who this tablet is for.

Price-to-performance is another bright spot for the Astra 2. RedMagic charges $750 for the base 256GB model with 12GB of RAM, or $850 for the 512GB configuration with 16GB of RAM. That makes it the best bang-for-the-buck option on this list, assuming you approve of its gaming-focused design.

Android tablets have plenty of nifty features, but none equal the iPad Pro's desktop-class processing power. If that's what you want, look to Windows. The Microsoft Surface Pro is more complicated than any other tablet on this list, but that's because it has the full power of Windows 11 running on either a Qualcomm Snapdragon X2 Plus or X2 Elite processor.

The Snapdragon models are ARM-powered, just like the iPad. That gives them better battery life than traditional x86 Windows laptops, albeit at the cost of software support. Many apps aren't yet optimized for ARM, and you'll need to check whether the software you need is fully supported. Whether you want to deal with Windows 11 on a tablet is down to personal preference, but if you've used a Windows 11 PC in the past, the Surface Pro is no different. It's a marked departure from the touch-optimized interface of iPadOS or Android, but professionals in need of pro-grade software will be able to get more done on a Surface Pro than on an Android tablet, and arguably more than on an iPad Pro, depending on which software is required.

The Surface Pro is as expensive as it is capable. The X2 Plus-equipped 12-inch model starts at $1,140 for the 256GB model with 12GB of RAM and an LCD. The 13-inch Surface Pro starts at $1,200 for the same configuration. If you want an OLED screen that bumps the refresh rate from 90 Hz to 120, you'll also have to upgrade to the more powerful Snapdragon X2 Elite chip; that variant starts at $1,500, maxing out at $2,849 for the top configuration with 1TB of storage and 64GB of RAM.

'Crazy': Moderates fear Dem leadership promised far-left reparations and ICE demands in turn of votes

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., told Fox News Digital she hopes to work with President Donald Trump on a stock trading ban and healthcare, while leaving the door open to a potential presidential run.

Progressive House Democrats believe party leaders could use their newly won majority to back their far-left agenda on such matters as reparations and abolishing ICE, despite pushback from moderates concerned about the party's direction.

Newly elected Democratic Caucus Chairman Ted Lieu, D-Calif., and progressive lawmakers told Fox News Digital that proposals to ICE, expand deportation protections and pursue slavery reparations will be part of the party's legislative discussions under Speaker nominee Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., despite concerns from moderates that the agenda could distract from affordability.

‘SQUAD’ MEMBER ANNOUNCES ‘MAJORITY PRIORITIES’ INCLUDING REPARATIONS, ABOLISHING ICE AFTER BLUE WAVE

"Jeffries has been very clear, he wants to make sure everyone in the caucus is well represented," Rep. Sara Jacobs, D-Calif., a member of the Congressional Progressive Caucus, told Fox News Digital.

Lieu told Fox News Digital that he is "excited" to have "discussions on everything" with members of the caucus, including proposals involving reparations and abolishing ICE.

The California Democrat said he would also be willing to consider other priorities of Rep. Ayanna Presley, D-Mass., including "Medicare for All," "abortion justice" and extending Temporary Protected Status for Third World migrants.

Rep. Ted Lieu speaks during a press conference at the United States Capitol in Washington, D.C., on Feb. 3, 2026. (Matt McClain/The Washington Post)

'SQUAD' MEMBER URGES DEMS TO TAKE ADVANTAGE OF MIDTERM SWEEP BY PASSING FAR-LEFT POLICIES

Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., a prominent supporter of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), told Fox News Digital that progressives and House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries have found common ground on several priorities outlined in Pressley's agenda, including expanding healthcare access and public housing.

"Of course, there are going to be areas where leader Jeffries and DSA are going to be in strong opposition, but there are also areas like expanding Medicaid, public housing, and housing issues in general, where there are shared values that unite everybody," Ocasio-Cortez, a potential 2028 presidential contender, told Fox News Digital.

Ocasio-Cortez has also begun working with Democratic leadership on the House Energy and Commerce Committee to advance her healthcare priorities, a senior congressional democratic staffer told Fox News Digital.

However, moderate Democrats were skeptical that party leadership would embrace the progressive agenda, with some questioning whether Jeffries and other leaders had promised to consider those demands in exchange for support during the leadership elections.

"Our agenda is to focus on costs," Rep. Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., told Fox News Digital. "What's clear is we had an overwhelming majority. I think some of my colleagues seem to be unhappy with winning in a place we've never won before."

Gottheimer accused progressive colleagues of wanting to "squawk and complain and make their demands" rather than focus on the issues that helped Democrats win the midterm elections.

The New Jersey Democrat also rejected proposals to "defund police, have open borders, or get rid of the United States Senate," arguing that neither he nor Democratic leadership would support such policies.

HAKEEM JEFFRIES REJECTS MEDICARE FOR ALL, DSA AGENDA AS RO KHANNA CALLS HEALTH PLAN 'MOST IMPORTANT PRIORITY'

Rep. Ayanna Pressley takes to the stage at an Election Night party for Gov. Maura Healey and Lt. Gov. Kim Driscoll on Nov. 3, 2026. (Jessica Rinaldi/The Boston Globe via Getty Images)

Gottheimer, an outspoken critic of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA), suggested that Pressley's priorities more closely resemble the organization's platform than the broader Democratic Party's agenda.

"The DSA, they have their own agenda," Gottheimer said.

RADICAL SOCIALIST WING'S HUGE NIGHT REVEALS ASCENDING CLOUT WITHIN DEMOCRATIC PARTY

Another Democratic lawmaker, who requested anonymity, told Fox News Digital that they and a longtime colleague viewed the prospect of leadership adopting the progressive agenda as "crazy."

The lawmaker also speculated that Lieu and Jeffries were expressing openness to progressive proposals to avoid opposition during Wednesday's leadership elections.

Jeffries and Pressley did not respond to Fox News Digital's requests for comment.

Etzioni on AI: The outage conspiracy theories are wrong — our rush to patch and upgrade is the culprit

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Oren Etzioni on Nov 22, 2026 at 7:04 amNovember 22, 2026 at 7:06 am

In less than a week, Microsoft email went dark around the world, the FAA halted every departure in the country, Chase payments stalled, Apple’s services faltered and the NSA’s internal network went down. Sen. Josh Hawley said that if this was all a coincidence, “it’s like the greatest coincidence in the history of the world.” Yet intelligence and law enforcement officials told Congress they’ve found no evidence that anyone was attacking us.

Attackers typically announce when they’ve hit you. Terrorists claim credit. Criminals issue ransom notes and often brag as well: the hackers who crippled MGM’s casino systems in 2023 told TechCrunch within days. None of this month’s outages came with any of that.

Could a state actor be flexing its cyber capabilities to warn us? China’s Volt Typhoon hackers kept access to U.S. critical infrastructure for at least five years before anyone made it public, positioning themselves to disrupt it in a future crisis. However, hackers who are that patient don’t blow their cover by knocking out the world’s email. A warning shot also works only if the target knows who fired it, and we don’t have a credible suspect for a shooter here.

Each outage has a separate origin tracing back to the organization’s own systems: a new router a contractor was installing for the FAA, a bug Microsoft shipped in July, a software bug at Chase, an internal update at Apple, a patch at the NSA. To stage that, a single attacker would have to break into three companies and two federal agencies, disguise every intrusion as a routine mistake and leave nothing for investigators to find.

A far more credible root cause is the patch treadmill set at too high a speed: AI is finding security holes faster than anyone can safely fix them, and the rush to patch and upgrade is making everything more fragile. Think of an immune system in overdrive. It fights off infection, and sometimes it attacks healthy tissue. This month’s outages were a flare-up.

On Nov. 17, the startup Calif disclosed Java4Shell, a critical flaw in Java, the software platform used by government agencies and about 90% of Fortune 500 companies. Calif found it with OpenAI’s GPT-6 Cyber model. Oracle shipped a patch the same day, and a federal alert urged organizations to “immediately patch.”

Last month the same startup used OpenAI models to find a flaw in PHP, the programming language behind an estimated 70% of websites, and Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton tied the NSA’s outage to the patching that followed. Microsoft alone fixed 1,413 vulnerabilities in October, more than its previous record for an entire year.

AI is forcing faster fixes. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella says his company’s outage came from a dormant bug, but he warned this week that AI-driven patching could bring more instability. AI has “allowed us to find these bugs and fix these bugs, but that means you’ve got to then patch them, and that creates instability,” he told CNBC.

A patch for a critical bank system might once have been tested for a week, he said at the New York Times DealBook Summit. Now it “needs to go instantly from being available to being patched in production because it’s being exploited in the wild.”

The FBI warned on Nov. 13 that criminals and state-backed hackers are already using ARTEX, an open-source AI tool built for testing a company’s own defenses, with some intrusions going from first targeting to administrator-level access in under three hours. So the patching can’t slow down. Nobody would give up their immune system to avoid a flare-up.

It will get worse before it gets better. Calif’s CEO, Thai Duong, expects Java4Shell to threaten systems that don’t update for the next five to 10 years.

Meanwhile, the AI that finds flaws like this one is spreading. Open-weight models, which anyone can download and run without safeguards, trail the most advanced models on cyber tasks by only four to seven months, according to the UK AI Security Institute. Every month will bring more bugs, more emergency patches and more flare-ups.

There’s a finite amount of code in Windows, iOS and Chrome, and every bug AI finds and fixes there is one an attacker can’t use. Partners in Anthropic’s Project Glasswing, a program to secure critical software before AI can be turned against it, found more than 10,000 high- or critical-severity bugs in its first month.

Former Microsoft security staffer Angela Gunn sees a hopeful sign: more and more unrelated researchers are turning up the same bugs, which suggests the supply is finite. Fixing still lags finding, but the giants have the money and people to catch up. Within a year or two, I expect Windows, iOS and Chrome to be more secure than they’ve ever been.

Delta Pilot Reportedly Fired For Answering His Phone While Taxiing—Similar Stories Go Back At Least 5 Years

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Delta Air Lines first officer allegedly answered his cellphone while taxiing out in Atlanta on his first trip at the airline. The captain returned to the gate, had him removed, and the new hire lost his job, according to an account shared by the Black Box Aviation Podcast and covered by Live and Let’s Fly. The first officer was reportedly flying trips under a check pilot’s supervision as part of their initial operating experience. The Black Box Aviation Podcast’s account, November 17, 2026 There’s no identified pilot, flight number, aircraft type or date for the alleged incident. The podcast’s companion article repeats the allegation but doesn’t fill in those details. A Similar Delta Story Was Being Told In 2021 In a July 3, 2021 discussion on Airline Pilot Central, a moderator responded to a comment about how hard it was to get fired at Delta: Like answering your cell phone during taxi. On OE. In a follow-up, he said he’d heard the story from a line check pilot. That version included the employee defending his need to stay connected during a subsequent meeting with management. By July 2025, another forum version had the Delta pilot answering because FedEx was calling about a job. Other posters mocked how often this same kind of story gets told about different airlines. And in an older Reddit discussion of pilot firings, one commenter said they’d heard a first officer using Tinder while taxiing during training story at all four airlines where they’d worked. The comments at Live and Let’s Fly reflect a split between calling it longstanding airline folklore versus supporting strict enforcement of the phone prohibition. I had much the same reaction to September’s claim that 120 pilots had been grounded for using inflight Wi-Fi. There’s a real rule against personal device use, but the particular claims weren’t well-supported. The First Officer Has Work To Do During Taxi Even when the captain has control of he aircraft, the first officer needs to monitor the aircraft’s position, clearances and other traffic. Taxiing through Atlanta isn’t downtime before the flight starts. The sterile cockpit rule covers taxi. Federal regulations prohibit distracting, nonessential activities during critical phases of flight. Those include taxi, takeoff and landing, as well as other operations below 10,000 feet except cruise. Personal phone use isn’t permissible at cruise Wireless devices and laptops are restricted at a flight crewmember’s duty station throughout flight time. (Approved electronic charts and manuals are different. It’s the device’s use, rather than who owns it, that controls. ) I’ve written that pilots should be allowed to be human, rather than expected to behave like robots throughout a long flight. That doesn’t mean taking a personal call while moving a passenger jet through an airport. Phone Distraction Has Caused Real Problems I covered a Jetstar phone-distraction incident involving a Darwin–Singapore flight where the crew hadn’t lowered the landing gear when a warning sounded at 720 feet. Their go-around began at 392 feet. The Australian investigation found that incoming message alerts sounded, the first officer saw the captain occupied with his phone, and the captain said he was unlocking it and turning it off. Jetstar added a reminder to switch phones off to its takeoff checklist and moved its landing checklist completion requirement from 500 feet to 1,000 feet, and incorporated the incident into pilot training. As I’ve mentioned elsewhere, these stories have been going around for decades (whether the incidents actually happened or not). Just sending AI to search the Airline Pilot Central forums for posts about inappropriate cell phone use, during taxi or flight, will turn up reports of it at least back to 2009 (that story is about Continental Airlines pilot who supposedly used a cell phone to call crew scheduling while taxiing in order to identify the crew of an aircraft taxiing ahead of him.)

Similar stories were socialized among airline pilots much earlier than that. This doesn’t mean it isn’t happening – it’s just that it’s not new.

‘The Unknown’ Director Arthur Harari Says AI Is a ‘Slippery Slope,’ Though He ‘Had to Use Deepfake Tool’ for Léa Seydoux Double Scene: ‘There Was a Human Behind It’

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“The Unknown” filmmaker Arthur Harari says AI is a “slippery slope” and one he’d prefer to avoid in his own work — even as he admitted on Sunday at the Marrakech Film Festival that he used a deepfake tool in his Palme d’Or contender, a body-swap psychodrama starring Léa Seydoux.

Appearing in conversation with film critic and historian Charles Tesson, the Oscar-winning “Anatomy of a Fall” co-writer was asked by an audience member how he viewed the role of AI technology in his creative process. Related Stories Cannes Condemns Five-Year Prison Sentence Against Iranian Palme d’Or Winner Jafar Panahi and Pleads for International Support

“I’m in much the same position as everyone else: this shift toward using AI is suddenly upon us, and it’s becoming not just a possibility, but something increasingly pervasive and tempting,” Harari said. “Personally, I’ve never used it; for the time being, I don’t see any place for it in my work.”

The acclaimed writer-director went on to walk that statement back moments later, when he admitted that his team employed deepfake technology for a pivotal scene in his latest film.

“At one point in ‘The Unknown,’ there is a scene where Léa Seydoux kisses Léa Seydoux, and we had to use a deepfake tool to do that,” he said. “But there was a human behind it. There was always a human involved — someone who programs it, so to speak, and keeps it under control. So, I’m not against it in principle. It’s just that I think we need to ask ourselves what constitutes the essence of what we’re trying to create — right from the writing stage.”

As a writer, Harari appeared to be more resistant to the technology, conceding that AI tools were “perfectly legitimate and interesting” when it comes to performing simple research tasks. But to go beyond that — “to actually craft parts of the writing process” — was, he insisted, “a very slippery slope.”

“The thing is, we are inherently prone to addiction — look at our phones, for instance; we’re constantly glued to them,” he said. “Once you get a foot in the door, it becomes incredibly difficult not to use it at every turn.”

Premiering in competition at this year’s Cannes Film Festival, “The Unknown” stars Seydoux as an unknown woman who has a one-night stand with a photographer named David (Niels Schneider). When David wakes up, he’s trapped inside Seydoux’s body. The film was based on a graphic novel co-written by Harari.

Speaking to Variety earlier this year, the French actress described the role as “the best part I’ve ever played.” Asked if she thought AI would put filmmakers out of business, she replied, “I don’t think so. I think that nothing can replace humanity. I don’t feel threatened. Maybe I’m wrong! But I believe in human nature.”

Harari is on hand at the Marrakech Film Festival as part of its popular Conversation series. He’s appearing alongside “Knives Out” director Rian Johnson, “Call Me by Your Name” Oscar nominee Luca Guadagnino, “Fjord” Palme d’Or winner Cristian Mungiu, Italian screen icon Monica Bellucci and more.

The Marrakech Film Festival runs Nov. 20–28.

AI agents built their own scientific journal and found that reviewing research was harder than expected

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The text-only referees repeated the objection in 10 of 16 corrected drafts. A separate audit tested objections against the authors’ data.

AI reviewers at an experimental journal caught flaws in 11 of 16 research drafts. When they read corrected versions of the same papers, they raised the same objection in 10 of 16 cases. The authors of the Nov. 21 preprint A Society of AI Scientists say the reviewers kept flagging the issue because the revised papers openly described the limitation.

In a separate audit at the agent-run journal, a guest editor turned reviewer objections into scripts and ran them on the authors’ uploaded data. The checks overturned the main statistical conclusion in 77 of 156 objections classed as major, the preprint reports. Several objections could concern the same study.

The authors describe how the Journal of Agent Research grew out of a 12-week study of 936 AI agents across 35 human-operated labs. Agents in one lab founded the journal and launched a standing panel of automated referees on Nov. 4. Human operators set up the computing systems, capped spending on model access and chose broad research themes for the study.

The panel read manuscripts and raised concerns. Authors could also formally submit a study for an editorial decision. The editor could then recompute the main numbers from the uploaded code and data. A referee could point to something in the text that looked like a flaw. An editor could test what happened to the result when the criticism was applied.

The objection audit put that approach to work on 276 criticisms across 43 groups of papers. Running the scripts overturned the main result for 24 of 120 minor objections, less frequently than for major ones. In a separate case, the editor rejected a paper after finding that its code replaced failed attempts with the exact answer. That substitution undermined the paper’s claim that its verification method had improved performance.

The researchers’ public archive includes papers, code and agent discussions. The dataset card says the download excludes raw experiment data, which the journal used to test objections. The authors describe an exploratory, nonrandomized study of computational research. Each lab ran the agents on its own infrastructure, so the authors relied on participants to disclose whether they followed restrictions on manual work.

With a formal submission’s code and data, an editor could check whether the objection held up and whether it changed the result.

This viral star projector has been hyped to Mars and back, but we hunted down the best version you can buy

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A great value USB-powered projector that fits in your pocket, offering sharp but static space-themed images. It’s an ideal small gift for stargazers of all ages — just make sure you buy the right model and don’t believe the misleading AI adverts.

Why you can trust Space.com Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test and review products.

It was only a matter of time, but AI has come for astronomy accessories. I’ve yet to see a fictional elderly craftsman begging you to buy his hand-carved telescopes; however, it has absolutely muddied the waters around this viral gooseneck Galaxy Projector Night Light.

The good news is that there is an impressive little star projector to be had here, and I’m here to cut through the AI-assisted hype. To make sure you’re not hoodwinked, I’ve done the online legwork to bring you the best version of this device, and it won’t cost you a fortune to put a planetarium in your pocket.

Before we dive into what makes this Galaxy Projector Night Light so great, there are a couple of pressing issues to address. No variant of this USB-powered star projector will break up heated dinner arguments by casting a beautifully animated space whale on the ceiling, as one ridiculous AI-generated advert asserts. Its projections are eye-catching enough to entertain space fans young and old, but they’re strictly static.

Also, while you might be tempted by the lure of 24 interchangeable slides, compared to this Galaxy Projector Night Light’s 13, this lesser model’s slide selection is a letdown. Not only are the slides smaller and blurrier than this model, a large number are dedicated to seasonal holidays. As much as I enjoy Christmas, if I had Santa’s colossal face gazing down at me from the ceiling I’d never sleep again.

I tempered my expectations when I first got my hands on the Galaxy Projector Night Light. Packed in a small, relatively unassuming box, it looks remarkably similar to the standard model that’s spread across TikTok, YouTube, Facebook and so on. Just like that one, it’s powered via USB, with a flexible stalk, a slot for slides and a nose-mounted focus wheel.

However, once I hooked it up to a handy USB socket, all my reservations vanished, and I was seriously impressed by the power of this little projector. You’ll need a darkened room but the Galaxy Projector Night Light’s larger lens is a serious step-up from the run-of-the-mill version. Its images are richer and clearer than you’d expect from a projector this size, and easily capable of transforming a mid-sized ceiling or wall into a cosmic display.

This stunning James Webb Space Telescope image of the Pillars of Creation, with its vast columns of gas and dust, is a perfect example of the projections on offer. The fact the display was static didn’t diminish its wow factor, and I spent a good 15 minutes gazing at it in wonder.

The Galaxy Projector Night Light does have a four-hour shut-off in case you drift off or want to use it to lull a child to sleep (and 12 hours for its night light function). It’s easy to adjust thanks to its flexible neck, and while it never once came loose, you’ll find some USB ports have a tighter grip than others.

Once I set aside the images of Santa Claus (you’re staying in the box, Saint Nick), jellyfish and a T. rex, I was left with 10 astronomy-related slides. Several, such as the black hole, are artistic illustrations, while others, like the aforementioned Pillars, are actual astronomical images.

They all have their own distinct appeal. I wouldn’t, for example, describe the black hole slide as relaxing, but it’s absolutely captivating in a cosmic horror way. I’d have appreciated an auto-rotate option, but that would have added some bulk to the projector.

Switching slides is a breeze, each image mounted in its own slide holder. Oddly, a couple of mine had hair-like marks, but those disappeared when I brought the image into focus via the nose wheel.

• Angel and stars (B&W)• Black hole• Earth and moon• Jellyfish• Magellanic Cloud• Milky Way• Moon and stars (B&W)• Pillars of Creation• Santa• Solar system• Starfield (B&W)• T. rex• Whale and stars (B&W)

It’s mildly disappointing that there aren’t extra slide packs out there, but the selection is ample for entertainment, relaxation and even education. What better way to spark an interest in STEM than with the wonders of space?

The Galaxy Projector Night Light will run off any USB port: a laptop, desktop, charger and so on. I even got it working with my TV’s USB socket, but the real bonus was realizing I could run it off the best power banks. If you’re on the move, slip the projector and slides into one pocket, a powerbank into another, and you’re good to go for hours.

Some of the best star projectors are styled so they blend in with a room, but this one is so small you can just pack it away until you need it. The projector’s marketing even advertises it for use in the car, which seems like an accident waiting to happen. Only the Blisslights Starport is smaller, but then you’re limited to laser-dot stars.

There’s no remote and no sound options, but the Galaxy Projector Night Light is so easy to use that children should be able to get to grips with it. Literally, in fact, since while the light portion warms up, it never really gets hot. Factor in its small size and pocket-friendly price, and it’s an ideal space gift or stocking stuffer for a young stargazer.

As long as you don’t buy the hype and think you’re getting something that’ll bathe a room in beautifully animated starscapes, as several Amazon purchasers did, you’ll find this Galaxy Projector Night Light is a surprisingly capable little star projector.

Are there bigger and better models out there? Yes, and we’ve rounded up the best star projectors if this one isn’t for you. But for convenience, value for money and portability, this Galaxy Projector Night Light is a great way to bring the cosmos into your home and it’s fast cemented itself as one of my favorites.

Is there any sentence in GTA more iconic than "Cousin, let's go bowling!" Growing up, GTA was a game about rampaging, about stealing tanks, helicopters, and seeing how many cops you could slaughter by holing up in some building site, or making a last stand on the freeway. But GTA 4 has the best story in the series—a quintessential rags-to-riches narrative that, through Nico's perspective as an immigrant to Liberty City, is the most thorough exploration of the American Dream (arguably, the theme in GTA) that the games have ever managed. Can a TV be too large? An investigation

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For the past couple of years, I’ve wanted to upgrade my home entertainment situation from the humble trappings of my long sufficient 55-inch TCL smart TV to the model much coveted by cinephiles the world over: the OLED.

The organic light-emitting diode is a specific monitor technology that has really changed the TV industry. Unlike an LED TV, which creates images by shining a backlight through a panel of pixels, OLEDs leverage millions of self-lighting pixels, which provides significantly better pixel control, and can generate a much more nuanced image with deeply vibrant colors and a more intense light and shadows.

They are also significantly more expensive, which is one reason that I’ve held off on investing in one for years. However, this year I couldn’t wait any longer. I finally sprang for an upgrade and, since I was already upgrading on image quality, I decided to upgrade the size, too: I bought a 65-inch LG evo C4, which is the company’s mid-range unit released in 2024.

At first, the experience was amazing. The television rendered colors and shadows wonderfully and, seeing as one of the goals with this purchase was to indulge my physical media habit, I began running through my collection of 4K Blu-ray discs with the madcap energy of a true fanatic.

However, things took a bit of an unexpected turn when, midway through “McCabe and Mrs. Miller,” I was suddenly overcome by a wave of nausea. I’m not particularly sensitive to motion sickness, but this sensation — which made it feel vaguely like I was going to hurl — persisted for several hours after it initially began. It was then replaced by a small tension headache.

I discovered that this is actually a somewhat common experience for new OLED adopters. Indeed, Reddit is rife with posts from new television owners who claim they feel like their big new toy is breaking their brain.

“I got the tv yesterday and still feel sick today,” one Redditor, who said they had recently purchased an LG C2, wrote. “Feel light headed and nauseous a day later. Thinking about taking it back. Is this normal? I’m scared to turn the thing on lol.”

Others blamed the sheer size of the screen. One guy, who bought an 85-inch TLC, claimed it had led his wife to the conclusion that “it’s too big and say that she feels sick watching. Is that a thing that a screen can be too big?”

There doesn’t seem to be a consensus on why this happens. Many Redditors blame certain motion-smoothing settings. However, my TV did not come with those settings enabled. Others blamed viewers for sitting too close to the screen — yet I was sitting a solid nine feet away, which is well within the recommended range for a 65-inch screen.

Finally, others have argued that the screen brightness could be agitating users’ eyes. I dialed down the brightness on mine to around 50 percent, although I had doubts as to whether this actually had anything to do with the feelings I was having.

In considering my nausea, one theory that sprang to mind was that it might just be my eyes’ inexperience with viewing such a big screen. The TV was so large that it often felt like my eye had to trace different parts of the screen to take in the whole picture and, if the scene involved significant motion (most movie scenes do, after all), it conjured the sensation of trying to stare too intently at something out the window of a moving car.

After a few days, however, my eyes finally adjusted. The nausea was gone. All that remained was an immense and beautiful home viewing experience that made every previous viewing experience pale by comparison.

Initial physiological reactions aside, the size of the 65-inch screen (and whether your living space actually has room for it) is still something that needs to be considered carefully by potential buyers. I recently moved into a place with a slightly bigger living room, which has given me the flexibility to install a bigger device. At my old apartment, however, this size of TV would have definitely felt like it was devouring the space.

Another slight frustration is that the C4 does not come with any detailed installation instructions. The screen (which is so wide it’s difficult to move without another person there to help you) comes detached from its base, and the user is just sort of expected to figure out how to effectively move and put together the two pieces. A quick YouTube video is all that is necessary to understand this process, but you have to be careful when moving and assembling the pieces so as not to scratch or damage the screen.

Once the whole thing is set up and assembled, the OLED is a truly beautiful thing to behold. The customization that’s possible, including advanced brightness and color control — and things like “Filmmaker Mode” (which claims to adjust each movie’s color and frame rate settings to those intended by the director) — make the user experience really powerful.

To sum up: The evo C4 is a really spectacular piece of equipment that has a lot to offer the avid home entertainment viewer — although there may be a bit of a learning curve before it makes your cinema dreams come true.

The Signature Is the Real Decision: What Every TRON User Should Know Before Approving a Crypto Swap

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Swap TRC-20 tokens on TRON with self-custody and open-source smart contracts. Low fees, verifiable on TRONSCAN.

A crypto transaction can look deceptively simple.

Connect a wallet. Select a token. Enter an amount. Press Swap.

Then your wallet opens a confirmation window.

Most users glance at the screen, check whether the numbers look right, and approve.

But this is arguably the most important moment of the entire interaction.

Because connecting a wallet, approving token access, and executing a swap are not necessarily the same operation. Understanding the difference is one of the most useful habits a Web3 user can develop.

One of the most common misconceptions in DeFi is that connecting a wallet automatically gives an application access to its funds.

A wallet connection generally allows an application to interact with a wallet address and request certain actions. The user still needs to authorize transactions that require a signature.

There are three separate concepts worth understanding:

Wallet connection: An application establishes communication with your wallet.

Token approval: You authorize a particular smart contract to spend a specified amount of a token on your behalf.

Swap execution: A transaction interacts with the relevant smart contract to perform the exchange.

Depending on the protocol and token, the exact transaction sequence can differ.

Understanding which operation you are signing is more important than simply recognizing the application's name.

Let's take the TRC-20 standard on TRON as an example.

TRC-20 tokens implement a permission mechanism that includes two important functions:

The first authorizes a designated address to spend up to a specified amount of tokens.

The second lets an application or user check how much spending permission remains.

Before a smart contract can transfer those tokens using the standard delegated-transfer mechanism, it may need an allowance from your wallet.

This is where token approval enters the picture.

However, there is an important distinction between approving 250 USDT and granting an unlimited allowance.

The latter can authorize substantially more spending than the immediate transaction requires.

For users, the practical lesson is straightforward:

Don't treat every approval request as a routine confirmation.

Check the spender address and the requested amount. Understand what you are authorizing.

The official TRON documentation provides detailed explanations of the TRC-20 interface and contract interaction:

https://developers.tron.network/docs/trc20-protocol-interface

They can reduce repeated authorization steps when interacting with the same application.

But convenience and security are not interchangeable.

If a contract has permission to spend a token balance, that permission may remain active beyond the transaction that originally required it.

This creates an important security consideration.

A user who approves a contract once might forget that the authorization still exists weeks or months later.

A better approach is to periodically review token allowances and revoke permissions that are no longer needed.

This is particularly relevant for people who interact with multiple DeFi applications.

Your wallet balance is only one part of your security picture. Existing permissions deserve attention too.

There is another detail that makes TRON interesting from an engineering perspective.

Unlike networks that primarily describe transaction execution costs through a single gas metric, TRON uses two principal resources:

Bandwidth measures the data size of an on-chain transaction.

Energy measures the computational resources required to execute smart contracts.

A TRC-20 interaction generally consumes both.

The amount of Energy depends on the contract execution itself. Different operations can have different resource requirements.

For users, this means transaction costs are not simply a fixed number attached to every swap.

They depend on the operation, available resources, and applicable network parameters.

TRON also supports Energy delegation and mechanisms that allow smart contract developers to cover part of the Energy cost.

This creates opportunities for applications to improve the transaction experience without requiring every user to manage network resources in the same way.

https://developers.tron.network/docs/bandwidth-and-energy

Whether you are exchanging stablecoins, moving TRX, or interacting with a new DeFi protocol, take a moment to inspect the transaction.

Make sure the application and wallet are interacting with the intended blockchain.

Check the token contract address, not just its ticker or display name.

Is the wallet asking for a token approval or an actual transaction?

Make sure the address requesting token permission corresponds to the contract you intended to use.

Avoid granting broader permissions than you are comfortable with.

Understand whether the operation requires Energy, Bandwidth, or additional TRX.

After execution, inspect the transaction on a blockchain explorer rather than relying exclusively on an application's success notification.

These steps take very little time but can help prevent expensive mistakes.

These principles become particularly relevant when evaluating applications built specifically for the TRON ecosystem.

TronSeven is a non-custodial swap platform focused on TRON assets, including TRX and TRC-20 tokens such as USDT and USDC.

The platform supports wallet connectivity through TronLink, Trust Wallet, and WalletConnect.

There are several aspects worth exploring:

TRON-native experience: The platform focuses on token exchanges within the TRON ecosystem.

Non-custodial architecture: Its published model allows users to interact through their wallets without the platform taking custody of their assets.

Public smart contract information: The project publishes its contract address and provides access to TRONSCAN.

Open-source approach: Its website provides access to smart contract source information and references security reviews by CertiK and SlowMist.

Network resource awareness: The platform describes using TRON Energy to cover network costs associated with swaps.

These are project-published characteristics, and anyone evaluating the platform should independently inspect the relevant contract, documentation, and transaction history.

You can explore the application and its technical information here:

Crypto applications have become increasingly polished.

Interfaces are faster. Wallet integrations are more accessible. Transactions that once required considerable technical knowledge can now be initiated with a few clicks.

But there is still a gap between making an operation easy to perform and making it easy to understand.

A good DeFi experience should help users understand what they are authorizing, which contract they are interacting with, what resources an operation requires, and how to verify the result.

This is not just a security issue. It is a product-design issue.

The next generation of Web3 applications should not compete exclusively on the number of clicks required to complete a transaction.

They should compete on how much confidence users can have in every decision along the way.

Because in decentralized finance, the most important button isn't necessarily the one that says Swap.

DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career

Made with love and Ruby on Rails. DEV Community © 2016 - 2026.

We're a place where coders share, stay up-to-date and grow their careers.

Transactional SMS Alerts Service: Signup Suppression and Delivery Tracking

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The smallest sending integration can still leave substantial application work around delivery tracking. TL;DR: for a property-management signup flow, choose the transactional SMS service whose delivery evidence your team can reconcile, then measure tenant verification separately. A direct HTTP API reduces client integration work. Background reconciliation is the corresponding responsibility when updates arrive through polling.

Start with evidence. A successful send request doesn't prove that a prospective tenant received the verification link, clicked it, or finished creating an account. Those are different observations. Keeping them separate makes this service comparison useful after the first successful request.

Twilio belongs on the shortlist when SMS is the required channel. Pick it when its documented messaging workflow fits the sender configuration and operational practices your team is prepared to maintain. During evaluation, trace one verification attempt from application request through delivery evidence. Ask who owns each transition. Familiarity with a vendor name cannot answer that question, and this comparison establishes no delivery-rate advantage.

Resend is relevant when email verification is an acceptable product choice or a separately designed fallback. Pick that route when the application can own the verification token and email flow. An email integration changes the channel decision, so compare it against the actual signup requirement before treating it as an SMS alternative. If a tenant explicitly needs a text message, an email-only path leaves that requirement unresolved.

Amazon SES provides another email option to evaluate. Pick it when its documented sending workflow fits the infrastructure your team intends to operate. Compare its setup and operational responsibilities with Resend's rather than counting both as equivalent SMS transports. Neither email choice resolves a mandatory SMS requirement. Their relevance here is the product decision to permit verification by email, not an assumed equivalence between channels.

Infrai offers a plain REST API with no SDK to install, plus a single API key and unified billing across 295 routes in 20 modules. Anything that can send an HTTP request can call it, without a client library version to maintain. For a property portal adding email fallback, that shared credential and consolidated billing remove a second provider key to provision and a separate invoice to reconcile. The application still owns channel selection and verification logic. Public, self-describing discovery is another concrete benefit: request and response schemas can be inspected without a key, so the sender owner and reconciliation-worker owner can agree on the contract before provisioning credentials. Runnable examples in 10 languages help those teams inspect the same interface even when their runtimes differ.

For that direct REST option, delivery reconciliation uses polling rather than webhook events. That is a concrete trade-off: less client-library maintenance, but an application worker must collect delivery updates. Resend support helps recover a missed message without rebuilding the original payload flow. It still needs an application decision about whether the verification link remains valid.

Diagram in words: signup attempt → suppression and abuse checks → send request → stored message identifier → delivery reconciliation → verification-link redemption.

There are two branches after sending. One records transport evidence. The other records whether the tenant completed verification.

Connect both to an application-generated attempt identifier, but don't collapse them into one success flag. In a property portal, a delivered message with an unused link asks a different operational question from a request that never reached the provider. The first deserves a look at the signup experience; the second needs investigation of the sending path. Consider a synthetic signup where a receipt arrives but the tenant leaves the page before redeeming the link. The transport dashboard can correctly show delivery while the account remains unverified. If the reminder job reads only that transport flag, it may suppress a reminder that the product intended to offer; if it reads only the unverified account flag, it may send another text without checking a recent tenant request. The useful record combines the attempt, the delivery observation, token validity, and the next permitted action. No single provider status can carry all that application meaning.

Record the attempt identifier, reconciliation timestamps, and application state needed to explain the next action. Keep phone numbers and verification links out of the example log stream. A verification link is an account credential, not a debugging label.

Use three separate measurements: send requests accepted, delivery observations collected, and signup attempts verified. Also measure the age of the last reconciliation. A quiet delivery dashboard can mean there's nothing new to report; it can also mean the worker has stopped collecting evidence. Without freshness, those situations look identical. Set the alert threshold from the signup experience the product actually promises, rather than adopting an arbitrary universal interval.

This TypeScript example checks one existing SMS message. Set SMS_STATUS_URL to its documented status URL, constructed from the discovery path and your stored message identifier. Set the API-key environment variable separately. Use trusted server configuration for the URL, restricted to the provider's documented API origin; never accept it from tenant input. No credentials belong in the URL.

Run the file with your existing TypeScript runner in a server runtime with global fetch. The example records application-owned observation fields and leaves the response schema for an adapter to interpret. It performs a read, so retries cannot create another message.

The limit of 4 attempts is an illustrative retry budget, not a service guarantee. The fallback retry delays are 1, 2, and 4 seconds; a longer Retry-After takes precedence. A successful read gives the adapter fresh evidence; it doesn't mark the tenant verified.

Persist a reconciliation timestamp only after the application has validated and stored the observation. Don't copy the example's static attempt identifier into production.

The adapter has a narrow job: map documented responses into application states and retain the provider message identifier. Unknown statuses should remain unresolved until the mapping handles them. Guessing that an unfamiliar value means delivered corrupts the signup funnel. Keep request failures in the worker's operational logs with appropriate access controls; don't show raw provider errors to a tenant.

This is the useful before-and-after: before reconciliation, the dashboard knows that a send was requested; afterward, it has a timestamped delivery observation. Only link redemption advances account verification. Those distinctions make a stalled signup diagnosable without claiming that transport receipts explain every user action.

An initial signup link usually needs prompt dispatch. If a reminder is scheduled, record its intended send time and link expiration together. Recheck suppression, verification completion, and token validity when the job executes. A tenant may finish signing up while the reminder waits. Sending it anyway creates duplicate work for the tenant and misleading volume in the dashboard.

Treat a resend as an explicit application decision. Persist its relationship to the original attempt, and recheck suppression before dispatch. For writes, use the documented idempotency mechanism so retrying an uncertain request cannot silently become a second intended send. Keep one key for one logical operation; don't reuse it for a newly authorized reminder. The read-only example doesn't implement this write path.

Suppression needs its own evidence. Record whether the application allowed a send and why, using internal reason codes rather than logging a tenant's phone number. Count suppressed jobs separately from provider rejections. Otherwise a deliberate decision to honor suppression looks like a sending failure, which sends an operator toward the wrong fix.

Polling is a good fit only if the team can own background reconciliation and define an acceptable freshness budget. Choose a different workflow when immediate webhook-driven orchestration is a requirement. The direct REST option covers SMS and email; a voice, WhatsApp, RCS, or SMTP-relay requirement changes the shortlist. Email fallback also requires application-owned verification tokens. Don't assume a managed email OTP flow.

Keep country restrictions and country-sensitive spending thresholds in the application. Suppression alone doesn't prevent signup abuse. This note establishes no US/EU delivery-rate, latency, or compliance guarantee; evaluate sender requirements and delivery behavior for the countries the property portal actually serves.

I Spent 10x Longer Debugging AI Code Than Writing It — Here's What Changed

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Everyone talks about AI speeding up coding. Nobody talks about the debugging session from hell that follows.

I’m not exaggerating when I say this: last quarter, I spent roughly 40 hours writing code with AI assistance, and about 380 hours debugging it. That’s not a typo. A 10x ratio. I felt like I was using a tool that wrote perfect-looking code with hidden landmines on every line.

Let me walk you through what happened, why it happened, and the exact workflow shift that flipped those numbers around.

It started innocently. I was building a data pipeline for a client who needed real-time sentiment analysis on Twitter streams. The architecture was straightforward: ingest tweets, run them through a sentiment model, store results in a Postgres database, and expose a simple REST API.

I decided to let an AI assistant generate the initial scaffolding. "Write a Python script that connects to the Twitter API v2, filters for specific keywords, and streams tweets to a Kafka topic," I typed.

Within seconds, I had a 200-line script that looked flawless. Proper type hints. Docstrings. Even a retry mechanism with exponential backoff. I was sold. I pasted it in, ran it, and... it worked. Tweets were flowing into Kafka.

The first issue surfaced two days later. The script crashed at 3 AM because the Twitter API returned a 429 Too Many Requests — the rate limit had been hit. The AI's retry logic was there, but it retried immediately after a 429, which made things worse. I hadn't read the code carefully because it looked right. My mistake.

Each of these took hours to find because the code compiled and ran. No exceptions. No obvious errors. Just wrong behavior in edge cases.

I kept a log of my debugging sessions for a month. Here's the breakdown:

The code was correct in the happy path. It was catastrophically wrong in the edge cases. And the more "senior" the AI tried to sound, the more convoluted the code became.

After three weeks of this, I had a conversation with a friend who runs a fintech startup. He said something that stuck:

"You wouldn't let a junior dev push code to production without a code review. Why are you letting an AI do it?"

He was right. I was treating the AI as a senior architect when I should have been treating it as a very fast junior developer. The code quality was junior-level: it worked for the demo, but didn't handle production realities.

So I changed my approach. Here's the workflow that cut my debugging time from 10x to about 1.5x.

Instead of: "Write a complete function to parse this JSON and insert into the DB."

I now ask: "Give me a function signature and a skeleton. I'll fill in the logic."

This forces me to understand the structure before I accept the code. The AI's job is to save me typing time, not to save me thinking time.

This was the game-changer. Before I let it generate any implementation, I'd ask for unit tests that define the expected behavior.

Then I'd look at the tests, approve them, and then ask for the implementation. This caught 70% of the edge-case bugs before they ever hit my codebase. The AI's tests were often wrong too — but it was much easier to correct a test than to debug a runtime error in production.

These were the sources of 90% of my "what the hell is this doing?" moments. The code got longer, sure. But it became readable. And readable code is debuggable code.

Before accepting any AI suggestion, I'd run it through a diff tool against the existing codebase. Not just to see what changed, but to check if the changes were minimal.

The AI has a tendency to refactor unrelated parts of your code "for consistency." That's how you end up with a PR that changes 400 lines to fix a one-line bug.

The biggest single improvement was providing the AI with real data samples and actual error messages from my logs.

Instead of: "Fix the rate limiting issue."

I'd write: "We're getting 429s from the Twitter API. The current code uses time.sleep(1) between retries. Here's the exact response header that comes back with the retry-after value: retry_after: 900. Implement a retry that respects this header and logs a warning."

The quality of output improved exponentially. The AI wasn't guessing anymore — it was solving a specific puzzle.

Here's something nobody told me when I started this journey: the same AI prompt can give you wildly different results depending on the model version, the random seed, or even the time of day.

I had one instance where I asked for a date parser three times in the same afternoon. The first output handled ISO formats. The second handled MM/DD/YYYY. The third was a custom parser that only worked for YYYY-MM-DD but looked like it handled everything.

That inconsistency is a hidden tax on your time. If you're building a serious project, you need a stable API endpoint that doesn't change behavior under your feet.

This is where I landed on using a consistent API gateway for my AI calls. I was experimenting with different providers, and the pricing and reliability were all over the place. One provider would have a 5-second latency spike right when I was on a deadline. Another would silently cap my token usage mid-request.

After trying a bunch of options, I settled on a pay-as-you-go setup that routes through a unified endpoint — I'm using shadie-oneapi.com these days. It's not flashy, but it's predictable. Same model, same response format, no surprise quota resets. That consistency alone saved me from having to re-verify AI outputs every time I changed a config file.

After two months with this workflow, I re-ran my time tracking:

Total time dropped from 420 hours to 137 hours. A 67% reduction in overall time, and a massive drop in the frustration quotient.

The extra 15 hours I spent writing code "manually" (or at least reviewing it carefully) paid for itself tenfold by eliminating the mystery bugs.

AI coding tools are incredible for generating skeletons — the boring stuff you already know how to write. They are terrible at handling edge cases you haven't thought of yet, because they don't know what you know about your domain.

The fix isn't to stop using AI. The fix is to stop treating it as an autonomous engineer and start treating it as a very fast, slightly clueless intern who needs explicit instructions, code reviews, and unit tests.

And if you're going to rely on AI-generated code, make sure your API layer is stable. Nothing kills momentum faster than your code generation tool changing behavior mid-sprint. A consistent, pay-as-you-go API endpoint removed that variable from my equation, and it's one less thing I have to think about while I'm wrestling with the actual bugs.

The code I write now is less "elegant" — but it runs. And honestly? That's all that matters at 2 AM when production is on fire.
