Hollywood’s Top 25 Legal Troubleshooters

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Criminal charges, custody fights, contested fortunes — meet the lawyers who handle the problems agents can’t negotiate away

In Hollywood, having a lawyer on speed dial can mean only one of two things. A deal is imminent, or a jury is deliberating. If it’s the latter, chances are the attorney on the other end of the line is on this list.

The 25 Legal Troubleshooters on these pages handle the problems agents can’t negotiate away: criminal charges, custody fights, contested fortunes and who gets to sell the Lakers. Their firms represent stars, moguls and heirs, although lately the L.A. Public Defender’s Office has been giving them unexpected competition. The lawyers themselves are attracting attention, too. Brian Steel has a Drake song named after him; Laura Wasser has a fashion campaign. Others spend their days explaining why a chatbot shouldn’t rewrite your trust.

All are worth keeping in your contacts. Just in case.

This firmspent the year representing famous clients facing charges of murder, rape and sex trafficking. Agnifilo and Geragos followed their defense of Sean “Diddy” Combs with Harvey Weinstein’s third trial, which ended in a hung jury, while another client, Oren Alexander, Kim Kardashian’s former real estate broker, was convicted of sex trafficking alongside brothers Tal and Alon. (Tal was represented by Deanna Paul, now a partner at Geragos & Geragos.) Friedman Agnifilo, meanwhile, helped take the death penalty off the table for Luigi Mangione, then pressed to dismiss his state murder case on double jeopardy grounds following his federal plea. But the firm’s most incendiary client this year has nothing to do with Hollywood: Winston Lee is one of seven former Cornell fraternity members accused in a civil suit of drugging and gang-raping a fellow student.

Actor Olivier Martinez had Baghdaserians in his corner during his headline-grabbing custody battle with ex-wife Halle Berry. In October, the lawyer secured a temporary restraining order and primary physical custody of their 13-year-old son. The judge later dissolved the restraining order but kept Martinez the primary custodial parent. Martinez is now seeking increased child support and $240,000 in legal fees from Berry. Another custody client: Basketball Wives’ Clayanna Warthen, whose dispute involved former NBA player Andre Iguodala.

Berk’s clients sit on both sides of criminal cases: She has represented stalking victim Jennifer Aniston and defended Harvey Weinstein. She says three-quarters of her practice involves preventing charges from being filed. But singer D4vd’s murder case was impossible to keep quiet. Berk defended him against charges of killing 14-year-old Celeste Rivas Hernandez before withdrawing Aug. 31, when he qualified for a public defender — a growing trend in Hollywood (see Alan Jackson).

Two music cases, two different codas. Bloom’s firm settled Lauren Pisciotta’s harassment and assault suit against Kanye West in July, ending a two-year fight. But former Danity Kane singer Dawn Richard’s suit accusing Sean “Diddy” Combs of sexual assault and physical abuse was dismissed because most claims were filed too late. Bloom’s targets also include the FBI: Eight Jeffrey Epstein survivors she represents sued the bureau for allegedly failing to investigate reports of his abuse.

After winning “Blurred Lines” for Marvin Gaye’s family, Busch is taking on artificial intelligence. In October, he sued Anthropic on behalf of Elvis Presley’s rights holders, including Authentic Brands Group, alleging its Claude models trained on 274 Presley songs without permission. The demand: up to $41.1 million — or about $150,000 per song. Anthropic disputes the claims. Similar suits for music publishers Reservoir Media and Round Hill have made Busch a recurring adversary for the AI company.

More than 10 years after his death, Muhammad Ali is still one of Hollywood’s most active clients, and Eisen is the lawyer guarding his name. In January, the U.S. Postal Service issued an Ali Forever stamp, a milestone for an estate that the firm’s trusts and estates co-chair has helped steer through years of name-and-likeness deals. His firm’s other clients: Marilyn Monroe, Elvis Presley and Albert Einstein.

Findling and co-counsel Brian Steel dismantled the government’s murder-for-hire case against rapper Lil Durk, winning acquittals on all five counts. Findling argued that Durk’s former assistant, testifying for the prosecution, was himself the “mastermind,” urging jurors to see a “tsunami of reasonable doubt.” Afterward, 50 Cent dubbed him the “Billion Dollar Lawyer.” His client list also includes Cardi B and President Donald Trump.

Liner Freedman; Meister Seelig & Schuster PLLC

Freedman wielded the machete and Schuster the scalpel for actor-director Justin Baldoni in his battle with It Ends With Us co-star Blake Lively. His $400 million countersuit failed, but her harassment claims were dismissed, and the two sides settled without a payout. Lively later won $407,000 for defending against Baldoni’s defamation claim — just 5 percent of her $8 million legal-fee request, which Freedman called a “significant victory.”

The lawyer for accusers of Harvey Weinstein and Sean “Diddy” Combs is now representing the woman at the center of the Cornell fraternity rape case. Giuffra’s suit alleges seven fraternity members drugged and gang-raped her and that Cornell failed her. As the case exploded online, his client faced threats, doxing and swatting, while supporters rallied under #IAmJaneDoe to protect her identity. New York Attorney General Letitia James is overseeing a criminal investigation.

An O.J. Simpson “Dream Team” alum, Holley represented pitcher Tyler Skaggs’ family in its wrongful death case against the Angels, arguing the team failed to stop an employee supplying players with drugs. The club settled during jury deliberations. Alongside Mangels, she also helped record label Del Records cut a proposed federal fine — for doing business with a sanctioned, cartel-linked concert promoter — from $6.7 million to $1.8 million. Restraining orders are also a specialty; the two have secured them for Cindy Crawford, Kaia Gerber and Lana Del Rey.

When gamers take their grievances to court, Kumar represents the companies behind the screens. She reps Microsoft and Mojang, creator of Minecraft, in suits accusing game companies of hooking children. Her clients also include Tinder and Hinge, for which she secured dismissals in two cases alleging the apps failed to remove user profiles and messages. She also helped technology trade group NetChoice keep key provisions of California’s age-appropriate online design law blocked on appeal.

Jackson, who has defended Harvey Weinstein and Kevin Spacey, exited as Nick Reiner’s attorney in January. That notorious patricide-and-matricide case is now being handled by what’s rapidly turning into Hollywood’s hottest new legal shop — the L.A. County Public Defender’s Office. Not only is public defender Kimberly Greene now representing Reiner, but her colleagues Walid Kandeel and Lisa Roth took over singer D4vd’s murder defense from Blair Berk in August. Jackson, meanwhile, is teaming with former client Karen Read for a true-crime podcast, Read Between the Lines, debuting in early 2027.

Hudson and Moses didn’t just get Justin Baldoni’s $400 million countersuit against Blake Lively and Ryan Reynolds thrown out; they helped stick him with $407,000 of Lively’s legal bill, securing the first federal court award under California’s law protecting sexual harassment accusers from retaliatory defamation suits. Moses helped draft the motion; Hudson led Lively’s team. Lively’s own claims ended in a settlement without a payout.

Nikas won $102.2 million for the Morgan Art Foundation against publisher Michael McKenzie over unauthorized and altered works based on Robert Indiana’s LOVE — the familiar sculpture with the tilted O. He calls it the largest art-litigation verdict on record. His partner Spiro had more mixed results with client Elon Musk: Delaware’s Supreme Court reinstated his roughly $56 billion Tesla pay package, but a San Francisco jury found he misled Twitter investors.

Some brothers fared better than others. Paul defended celebrity realtor Tal Alexander against charges that he and his siblings drugged and sexually trafficked women. The jury convicted all three, and each received a 30-year sentence. Geragos had more luck with the Lyle and Erik Menendez, helping them secure a new parole hearing roughly 17 months ahead of schedule after the brothers were denied release last year. His TMZ appearances stirred up some controversy, though, with Geragos’ comments on the Cornell fraternity rape case prompted the accuser’s lawyer to allege that he was effectively assisting the defense team, which includes his daughter, Teny, at Agnifilo Intrater. Geragos denies it.

He doesn’t represent Hollywood actors or battle for studios, but the 75-year-old Massachusetts attorney did become one of 2026’s biggest reality stars. His livestreamed defense of Lindsay Clancy, accused of killing her three children, inspired more than 16,500 TikTok videos under his hashtag, autograph requests outside court and fan-made “Better Call Kevin” merch. Reddington argued Clancy was not criminally responsible due to postpartum psychosis. The jury deadlocked in September.

After helping Britney Spears escape her conservatorship, Rosengart fought for FKA twigs to reclaim her voice, winning an agreement lifting restrictions on discussing her sexual abuse allegations against ex-boyfriend Shia LaBeouf. He’s also defending Selena Gomez against investors who accuse her of failing to deliver on commitments to build and promote her mental health startup, Wondermind.

Royce has handled splits for Arnold Schwarzenegger, Channing Tatum and Forest Whitaker. Now she’s representing Lori Loughlin, whose marriage to Mossimo Giannulli survived the college admissions scandal and prison terms for both but ended in separation after nearly 28 years of marriage, with Loughlin filing for divorce in September.

A letter from Marty Singer has ruined many a Hollywood morning. In November, he accused filmmaker Yi Zhou of harassing Jeremy Renner after she alleged the actor sent explicit messages and threatened to call ICE. He also represented Priscilla Presley against former business partners who accused her of concealing a $25 million jewelry collection; they dropped that suit in February, retaining the right to refile. In March, Lavely & Singer became Singer Weinsten Wolf & Jonelis, adding three longtime partners to the firm’s name.

Most lawyers hope for a referral. Steel got a Drake song named after him. After representing Young Thug and joining Sean “Diddy” Combs’ defense, the Atlanta attorney teamed with Drew Findling to win Lil Durk acquittals on all five counts in his federal murder-for-hire trial. Fans swarmed him outside court after the verdict. Durk remains jailed ahead of a separate racketeering trial in August 2027.

Strauss and Forster help Hollywood’s wealthy pass fortunes to the next generation, minimizing estate taxes and planning for the transfer of family businesses. Lately, some clients have acquired another adviser: a chatbot. Strauss told CNBC that clients are uploading trust documents to AI, then returning with suggested revisions that can take hours to review. “I have not actually received a single workable suggestion from that process,” he says. The firm is updating its contracts to warn that sharing confidential documents with AI “lawyers” could jeopardize attorney-client privilege.

After helping secure Lachlan Murdoch’s control of Fox and News Corp, Streisand moved to another family power struggle: Jeanie Buss versus her five siblings over whether to sell the family’s remaining 17.8 percent Lakers stake. Her brothers and sister want to cash out alongside billionaire Mark Walter, who bought control of the team in 2025 and has agreed to sell to Bob Iger and Joshua Kushner at a $12.5 billion valuation. Buss wants to retain the shares. A hearing is set for Dec. 8.

Wasser doesn’t just handle divorces; she models for them, fronting fashion label Reformation’s “Divorce Collection” campaign this year. For Sia, she filed an October settlement keeping the singer the primary custodial parent of her toddler with Dan Bernad, who receives $42,500 a month in child support. Her newest client is Hila Klein, who filed to divorce husband and H3 Podcast co-host Ethan Klein on Nov. 2 after publicly accusing him of an affair.

Wigdor is leaving no stone unturned in Julia Ormond’s ongoing sexual assault suit against Harvey Weinstein — pursuing Disney, Miramax and CAA for allegedly failing to protect her. His firm even won an order requiring former CAA chairman Michael Ovitz to answer questions under oath about the agency’s representation of the actress. Other clients include Cassie Ventura, whose rape and sex trafficking suit against Sean “Diddy” Combs settled a day after filing. The firm is also suing Leon Black, alleging the financier filed retaliatory lawsuits to intimidate lawyers representing his sexual assault accusers.

For Zwicker’s wealthy clients, moving away can be as complicated as passing a fortune down. Alongside estate planning for entertainers and multinational families, she advises on the tax consequences of changing countries. California, for one, doesn’t surrender its taxpayers easily. She told The San Francisco Standard that auditors examine ATM withdrawals and Apple Pay transactions to determine whether residents have really left — or just their tax bills. “They want to see all of the data on all of your devices,” she says.

How to deploy an app you built with AI to your own server

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 20 • Originally published at opscrew.dev

Originally published on the OpsCrew blog.

To deploy an AI-built app to your own server, you do five things: get the code into a Git repository, find out whether it is static files or a server, decide where its backend lives, build it into a container image with the right environment variables, and run that image behind a reverse proxy that serves your domain over HTTPS. Lovable, Bolt.new, Replit, v0 and Claude Code all take this path; what differs is the stack each one produces and the backend it leans on.

Every builder fact below comes from the builder's own documentation, checked on 11 November 2026. The Dockerfiles and the Caddy and Docker Compose files in the builder guides were built and run in Docker the same day, on Node.js 22, against fresh apps from the Vite, TanStack Start and Next.js starters and a small Express and PostgreSQL app.

Every builder hosts what it builds: Lovable publishes to lovable.app with custom domains and a managed backend, Bolt has Bolt Cloud hosting with .bolt.host addresses, Replit publishes to its own Google Cloud infrastructure, and v0 publishes to Vercel. Lovable's own docs recommend starting on Lovable, syncing the code with Git, and moving parts only when you hit a real constraint. Stay where you are when:

Move when you need something the builder's hosting does not give you: a fixed monthly price, data in a region or on hardware you choose, other services on the same machine, root access, or an app that no longer depends on one vendor. The comparisons of your own server against Vercel and against hosting platforms in general go through the trade-offs.

How each builder gets its code to GitHub, checked 11 November 2026

Then clone the repository and run the app on your own computer. If it does not start locally, it will not start on a server, and a local run shows you every environment variable it expects.

Open package.json and look at the dependencies and the build and start scripts.

The stacks AI builders produce and what each needs to run

Most builder apps keep their data and sign-in in a hosted service that the code reaches over the internet and that does not move with it:

Move the frontend first and keep the backend where it is. Once the app runs on your server against it, decide whether to move the data too, as a separate step with its own test.

Build-time public variables. Vite exposes variables that start with VITE_ to the browser and replaces them in the code at build time; Next.js does the same with NEXT_PUBLIC_. Their values end up inside the JavaScript every visitor downloads, so:

Runtime variables. Everything else, read on the server while the app runs: DATABASE_URL, API secrets, session secrets. These go into the container's environment on the server, from a file that never enters the repository. A server-rendered app can need a public value twice: a Lovable TanStack Start app reads its Supabase URL and key as VITE_ values in the browser and again as SUPABASE_URL and SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY on the server.

Collect both lists before you build. Lovable keeps its public Supabase values in the repository's .env; Bolt keeps environment variables in .env but its secrets only on Bolt's servers; Replit's secrets must be re-entered; v0 keeps everything in the Vercel project's settings.

The Dockerfile depends on the stack from step 2; each builder article has one that was built and run for that stack:

Every stack gets the same front door: a reverse proxy takes the public traffic on ports 80 and 443 and passes requests to the app. Caddy also handles the domain's HTTPS certificate by itself: when the domain's DNS record points at the server and ports 80 and 443 are open, it obtains and renews it and redirects HTTP to HTTPS. This Caddyfile is the whole configuration for an app that listens on port 3000:

This compose.yaml runs the app and Caddy together, with the certificate kept in a volume so it survives restarts:

Replace app.example.com with your domain; on the server, docker compose up -d --build builds the image and starts both. Build-time variables from step 4 go under build.args, as each builder guide shows. Only Caddy's ports are published to the internet, never the app's. We ran these files with localhost as the site address, where Caddy issues a certificate from its own local authority; a public certificate for a real domain needs a real server and DNS record, which this test did not cover.

Any Linux server with Docker works, including one you already pay for. The general steps are the same for every app: how to deploy a web app to a VPS covers creating the server, SSH keys, the firewall and Docker; Docker Compose on Hetzner with GitHub Actions and a DigitalOcean Droplet walk through two providers; and Hetzner vs DigitalOcean helps you pick one.

For the domain, add an A record (and an AAAA record if the server has IPv6) pointing at the server's address. Caddy can only get the certificate after that record resolves, so set it before the first start.

Once the app is live, add deploys without downtime, so an update does not drop visitors, and a preview for every pull request, which replaces the builder's preview link.

Check each of these on a temporary address while the builder's hosting keeps running. Once all pass, move the domain and unpublish the old copy when you are ready.

What breaks after you move an AI-built app, and why

Lovable apps, and Bolt apps whose database you claimed into Supabase, sign users in through Supabase. Its Site URL is the default redirect address that confirmation and password-reset emails use, and its Redirect URLs list says where users may be sent after sign-in. In a Supabase project of your own, both are on the dashboard's URL Configuration page; for Lovable Cloud, Lovable's hosting guide says only to add the new address to the auth provider's allowed redirect URLs.

OpsCrew is an open-source tool that takes an app from its GitHub repository to a hardened server on your own cloud account. It recognises code exported from Lovable, Bolt, Replit and v0, and its free opscrew scan checks the repository for leaked keys and exposed Supabase or Firebase data before you go live.

It builds the container image in GitHub Actions with its own Dockerfile for Vite and Next.js apps and Node.js servers such as Express (or yours, if the repository has one), and handles VITE_ and NEXT_PUBLIC_ values as build-time values that are public by nature. It deploys to a new server on Hetzner Cloud or DigitalOcean, or to a server you already have, buys or connects your domain on Cloudflare, and runs a database next to your app when you want one, or keeps the hosted one you already use. Every change waits for your approval of its exact plan. In Claude Code, the OpsCrew plugin runs the same flow from the chat. The setup page lists the accounts and tools you need, and use cases shows what it does for an app from an AI builder.

SpaceX Just Raised $32 Billion for AI Chips. Lenders Want Elon Musk to Keep Them on Earth.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SpaceX had to pay a yield above 8% on the biggest piece of a $32 billion bond sale to buy Nvidia chips.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 20, 2026 at 12:28PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Space Exploration Technologies Corp. (SPCX -7.02%) sold $32 billion of bonds through a trust set up to buy Nvidia (NVDA -6.81%) chips on Thursday, with the largest piece paying investors a yield above 8%.

And, according to reporting from The Financial Times, the lenders involved wanted guarantees that SpaceX would keep the financed chips on terra firma, apparently worried about the logistics of repossessing chips circling the Earth at about 17,500 mph.

The stock was down 6.7% as of 11:39 a.m. ET on Friday as the whole market sees red.

The bonds are part of a more than $40 billion financing package SpaceX announced in October. To try to keep the debt off its books, the financing runs through a separate entity -- a trust that sells the bonds, buys the chips, and leases them to SpaceX. Similar maneuvers are increasingly common across the AI data center market as companies look for ways to deal with ballooning debt balances.

Functionally, however, SpaceX is still the one borrowing, and its quarterly lease payments are what pay the bondholders back.

The largest piece, about $25.2 billion, was priced at a yield of 8.259%. The average yield on high-grade U.S. corporate debt was 5.95% that day, so investors are being paid about 2.3 percentage points more than usual for bonds that are supposed to be relatively safe. Bonds considered "junk" were yielding an average of 8.3% that day.

The loans are secured by the chips in the same way your mortgage is secured by your home. If SpaceX ever defaulted, the lenders' fallback would be to repossess the GPUs and sell them. That's obviously quite difficult if they're in space. It's not clear if the lenders were able to get anything binding in the final documents, but SpaceX says these chips are for its "terrestrial compute build-out."

The chips, destined for SpaceX data centers, are expected to start arriving in January. They're part of the massive build-out SpaceX's AI division is in the thick of, which has driven enormous growth on its top-line. The AI unit nearly tripled its sales between Q2 and Q3, growing revenue from $2.6 billion to $7.6 billion. Costs are high, however: the business spent $16.4 billion in capex in its Q3 -- the lion's share of the company's overall $19.6 billion capex spend.

While the terms are less favorable than past deals, SpaceX got the money it was seeking. CFO Bret Johnsen said in the company's October financing announcement that SpaceX is seeing "less than one year payback periods" on its infrastructure investments.

But I'm still skeptical long-term. Debt market investors are incredibly sophisticated, and watching spreads -- the gap between what a U.S. Treasury pays and what the bond yields -- widen for SpaceX doesn't do much to convince me that the economics make sense. Right now, the payoff looks incredible. I don't think that's going to hold up.

I've clearly not been playing the same Nintendo game

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Legend of Zelda fans have spent the past three years or so building wild contraptions in Tears of the Kingdom, from Sims-style houses to submarines – and, now, a military's worth of combat vehicles… functional combat vehicles.

Make that functional combat vehicles featured in a short film, too. If you've not seen them yet, a user known as Keke shared their creations on X, explaining that they've made a little movie of sorts from them. Attached is the neat video they compiled that shows their builds in action (and I mean in action, y'all), starting with a rolling tank. Yes, a tank. And, yes, they show it shooting as a real cannon would.

ティアキンで短編映画作りました。 　全編はYoutubeにて公開中。 【黒龍迎撃―ハイラル本土決戦―】 https://t.co/rF6Ug78rZONovember 14, 2026

"I made a short film in Tears of the Kingdom," they write, "The full film is now available on YouTube." If you go to YouTube, you'll find Keke's near half-hour long project, complete with a title: "DEMON DRAGON INTERCEPTION - HYRULE WAR." The title also makes clear that they didn't use any mods whatsoever. It isn't just tanks, either. There are fighter planes, massive military ships, and more – and there's no better word for it then "epic."

The comments are, unsurprisingly, positive, too, with folks noting that at times "it really looks like a battleship is firing, it's amazing." Others describe it as "peak gaming," which, honestly, checks out after seeing the footage. Taking on the Demon Dragon is one thing, after all, but taking it on in a self-built tank or jet is another, if you ask me. It makes my measly platform-wheel-fire contraptions feel… well, like garbage.

It's inspiring to see that the Tears of the Kingdom isn't slowing down, either, even after the likes of the Ocarina of Time remake and its own arrival. I suppose its capacity for creative expression is part of the reason why it's so long-lived – and I'm very much here for it.
